Before React Native, building mobile
applications for multiple platforms was
a nightmare. you had to write separate
code bases for iOS and Android, which
meant double the work, higher costs, and
slower development cycles. But with
React Native, you can write a single
codebase that works seamlessly on both
platforms, making development faster,
cheaper, and more efficient. And it's
not just me saying this. Big companies
like Meta, Discord, Microsoft, Tesla,
Amazon, Airbnb, and even Call of Duty
use React Native to build their mobile
apps. React Native has evolved so much
that you'll find fewer people fluttering
these days. And with the release of
React's new architecture, performance
has been improved significantly. So the
next time someone says, "Hey, React
Native apps are slow." Just take a deep
breath and say, "Not anymore, Flutter
Boy." Let me break it down. First,
there's the new JSI, JavaScript
interface, which replaces the old
bridge, allowing JavaScript to
communicate directly with the native
code for faster and smoother
performance. Then there are the new
turbo modules that load native modules
only when needed, reducing startup time
and memory usage. And third, there's a
new thing called fabric, which optimizes
UI rendering, making animations,
gestures, and updates faster and more
efficient. With JSI, Turbo modules, and
fabric, React Native apps are now as
fast and smooth as the native ones. And
aside from its new architecture,
cross-platform support, and native
components, React Native also comes with
hot reloading, allowing you to see the
changes you code instantly, a huge

in React Native world, Nativewind came

Isn't this crazy? It feels like you're
writing a regular web app, but instead
you're developing apps for mobile. Now,
let's talk a bit about the view
component. Think of it as a box or
container that holds other components.
Similar to the div element in HTML, but
with some added functionality specific
to mobile apps. The view component is
often used to create layout structures
for other components.
It has many different props that can be
used to control its appearance and
behavior. And one thing to note is that
the view component uses flexbox layout
by default, which makes it really easy
to control how its children components
are laid out. So you can use flexbox
properties like flex direction, justify
content, and align items to achieve any
layout you want. But what if you want to
add some interactivity to your React
Native apps? Well, get excited because
there are some amazing components that
do just that. Components for creating
buttons, links, and other interactive
elements. The first one on the list is
touchable opacity, which is great if you
want to create a simple button. Think of
it like a cousin to a React button
component, but with even more room for
customization. And instead of an on
click in React Native, you're not
clicking it, you're pressing it. So,
let's provide an on press. The second
similar component is called a touchable
highlight, which allows views to respond
to touch in a unique way. When touched,
the component reduces the opacity of the
wrapped div, revealing the underlying
color. And then there's the touchable
without feedback which is there if you
need to create an element that is
clickable but you don't want it to have
any visual feedback when pressed. It is
super useful when creating links or
images that don't need any additional
effects. And apart from these touchable
components, there's also activity
indicator which allows you to show a
spinner or a loading indicator within
your app. Sure, there's also a simple
button allowing you to set properties
like the title, color, and an on press
that is called when the button is
pressed, but whenever you need some more
advanced styling or behavior, you'll
find yourself using the touchable
components much more often as they offer
greater flexibility. Now, the next super
important component on the list of
components I want to teach you right now
and which we'll use later on within our
app is called a flat list. A flat list
is perfect for rendering long lists of
items that need to be scrolled. It's
like the map function in React, but with
some extra features like optimize scroll
performance and item separation. The way
it works is you of course import it from
React Native and then you define some
data such as an array of objects you
want to map over. Then you put it all in
a view and you call it flat list. A flat
list by default already accepts some
props such as the data prop to which you
can pass an array of data you want to
map over and then a render item prop
which allows you to define exactly how
you want to represent each item in the
array. Pretty cool, right? Now, when
should you use a flat list and when
should you just map over the elements?
Well, for larger lists where you want to
have smooth scrolling, go for the flat
list. While for the smaller lists, the
map function will do the job. There's
also something known as a scroll view.
You can think of it like a magic box
that can hold multiple components and
views, providing you a scrolling
container for them. It's like the Orflow
scroll property of a div in HTML,
allowing you to easily navigate through
a list of items or large amounts of
content in our app. You can put it
within a view, call it scroll view, and
then render some elements within it. By
using the scroll view component, you can
make sure that users can easily explore
all the content, making your app more
intuitive. And there's also a component
which you'll find yourself using super
often called safe area view, which gives
you some sort of a safe zone to render
your app's content within without it
being covered by the devices hardware
features like the notch, home indicator,
or a status bar. It's great for building
apps that are supported on different
devices with different screen sizes and
shapes. The way you use it is whenever
you think something might be too long or
awkwardly placed, you just wrap it

So a custom algorithm, right? Well,
technically yes. You'll implement a
feature that displays the movies that
the users are searching for within the
app. To be more specific, the more
frequently a specific search is
performed by multiple users, the higher
its trending status becomes. This
requires tracking and analyzing search
patterns over time. For example, if many
users search for Avengers, that search
term becomes trending. And to track and
analyze these searches, we need a place
to store that data permanently. This is
where a database comes into the picture.
It keeps all the information organized
and accessible. And traditionally,
implementing this would mean building a
full stack application. You'd have to
create a server, set up a database,
connect them, host everything together,
and then integrate it into your React
Native project. While this approach
works, it can be a lot of effort and
requires diving into back-end
development, which is a whole different
area of expertise. An easier way to get
back-end functionality without starting
from scratch, is using a back-end as a
service platform. It'll provide you with
APIs and tools to let you store your
data, manage O, and more without needing
in-depth back-end knowledge. In this
course, I'll use AppRight because it's

And to finish this course off, I want to
show you one little gotcha that I found.
It's those little things that you don't
find in the documentation very easily,
but rather it's a best practice that you
have to dig through GitHub discussions
to figure it out fully. I zoomed out
this code so you get a better
understanding of the entire homepage
that we have right now. Notice a couple
of things. We have a single scroll view
right here where we said show vertical
scroll indicator is set to false. But
doesn't matter that we're hiding the
indicator. This scroll view is a
vertical view. And within that vertical
view, we also have another flat list.
This one right here, which renders all
the movie cards. Those movies are also
in a vertical view. And it is not
generally recommended to have two
virtualized lists or scroll views or
flat lists in the same orientation. See,
this flat list is not a problem. This
one is horizontal, and that's okay when
it comes to the performance. But having
two huge flat lists that are of the same
orientation is generally not considered
a good practice. We're using a scroll
view to render most of the movies. And
if you go to the docs, it's going to say
scroll view versus flat list. Which one
should you use? Well, scroll view
renders all its children components at
once, but this has a performance
downside. Imagine you have a very long
list of items you want to display, maybe
several screens worth of content.
Creating JS components and native views
for everything all at once, much of
which may not be even shown, will
contribute to slow rendering and
increased memory usage. And this is
where the flat list comes into play.
Because flat list is smart, it renders
items lazily just before they're about
to appear. And it removes the items that
scroll way off the screen to save memory
and processing time. So in other words,
when you have large lists of vertical
elements, you want to use a flat list
and not a scroll view. And in our case,
if you look at the flat list, we
technically didn't allow it to reach its
full potential because we have set
scroll enabled to false, which
technically means that we let the scroll
view do its job. Even though it is
totally fine, perfectly fine for our use
case as we don't have many elements and
we wouldn't even been able to harness
the power of the flat list for bigger
applications. It makes a big difference.
And that's exactly why I wanted to leave
this for the end of this video. While it
is totally fine for smaller apps, for
larger apps, you just got to be smarter
and utilize everything that the software
allows you to do. In this case, React
Native's built-in components, like the
flat list, are super powerful, but you
got to know how to use them. Below this
lesson, I'll leave the improved codebase
for how the homepage should have looked
like if it had hundreds or thousands of
elements. So, if you want to check it
out, you can go ahead and do that. But
this is just one little optimization out
of hundreds or thousands that we go over
on JS Mastery Pro. So, check it out,
cancel at any time, or get a refund if
you don't like it. But given how many
resources we have in our knowledge base
and within our paths, it's hard not to

We have the new architecture enabled
which uses newer versions of React
Native for better performance. And I
actually want to take a second here to
explain what's happening. This new
architecture is also known as a
bridgeless architecture. Meaning that
before we had to had a bridge that would
communicate between JavaScript and
native mobile application code. This
communication was solely based on that
bridge leading to issues like
performance bottlenecks, delays, jerkier
empty frames, and so much more. But in
the new architecture, we don't have that
bridge at all. React Native has
introduced new methods like fabric for
rendering, turbo modules for native
modules, and JSI to allow direct
synchronous communication between
JavaScript and native code without the
bridge. With these, React Native
eliminates performance issues we had
with old arc and others. And the best
thing is that as we're talking about
this, Expo immediately supports this new
architecture without you needing to do
anything. And along this you can also
use the latest react features like
transitions. Moving ahead we have the
iOS specific features whether we want to
extend them on tablet devices and same
thing for Android specific properties.
Similarly we have some web specific
properties as well. Now coming out of

Now, if you try to test your app, you'll
notice that you can't scroll the whole
screen. Rather, you're only able to
scroll the flat list content. See how
the header remains there, but flat list
is scrollable. Why is that? Well, in web
development, the browser actually adds
scroll bars if the content exceeds the
viewport. But in React Native, you're
working in a native mobile environment
where the layout engine doesn't
necessarily assume that you want to
scroll. So you must explicitly wrap your
content in a scrollable container like a
scroll view or a flat list in this case.
If you want to add horizontal or
vertical scrolling behavior like we have
here. So in case you want to make the
whole thing scrollable, you can just
wrap it in a scroll view which you can
import from React Native
and make sure to properly close it right
at the bottom right inside of the safe
area view. If you do that, you can
notice that now the whole thing is
scrollable. But with this in mind, we
have one tiny problem. And that is that
if you open up your application
terminal, you'll see that it says that
virtualized lists should never be nested
inside plain scroll views with the same
windowing and other functionality. Use
another virtualized listbacked container
instead. This problem is happening
because a flat list which is a
virtualized list is designed to render
only the visible items for performance,
not the ones that cannot fit on the
screen. Wrapping it in a scroll view
though, which tries to render everything
at once, disables that optimization,
causing poor performance, layout
glitches, and broken scroll behavior. It
is a pattern that you don't want to do
in React Native. So, please never ever
wrap a flat list or any other
virtualized list inside of a scroll view
ever again because that's a React Native
rookie mistake number one. So, if you
want to avoid these mistakes in the
future, you'll definitely want to check
out our Pro React Native course. Now, it
depends when you're watching this video
whether it's out yet or not. But even if
it's not, somewhere here in the video
kit down below, I'll link a wait list
for the course so that when it's
actually out, you can be the first one
to know and never again make this and
many, many other common React mistakes
ever again. because in the course we'll
dive deep of how React Native works
under the hood. So what should you do
instead? Well, check this out. I'll
remove this scroll view
and its ending tag and we can render

In this case, apprite. In React Native,
store management can be handled using
libraries like Zustand, Redux, or just
React context API. They're all made to
solve the same problem, maintaining the
global state across the app. And in this
course, I'll teach you how to use
Zastand. Why? Well, it's a great choice
because it's lightweight, minimalistic,
and has a very simple to understand API
with zero boilerplate. No reducers,
action types, or a lot of that stuff
that Redux just throws at you. It's just
plain JavaScript. And unlike context, it
avoids rerender issues and is better for
performance. So let's go ahead and
simply install it by running mpm install

here. Great. So, you might think that we
have everything we need, but there's one
challenge here. Some customizations
apply only to burgers, but not to
pizzas, for example. Others apply to
both. This is a classic many to many
relationship. A menu item can have many
customizations and a customization can
belong to many menu items. And in
relational modeling, the cleanest way to
handle this is by introducing a join
table or in Apprite's case, a new
collection. This collection will be
called menu
customizations
and is essentially a joint table. It'll
contain pairs of references, one
pointing to the menu item and the other
to the customization. This architecture
will keep the data clean, reusable, and
consistent.
For example, a user might add a
pepperoni pizza with extra cheese and a
Coke to the cart. And because these
customizations exist as separate
documents, the app doesn't duplicate
that information. It simply links them.
I mean, you could say that you could
embed customizations directly within the
menu items that we create in our
database. It's a tempting shortcut, but
it quickly falls apart as the app grows
because embedding customization makes it
hard to update prices globally, leads to
inconsistent naming, and makes filtering
or analytics harder. By normalizing
data, separating it into reusable
collections. This structure is more
scalable and maintainable.
There's also flexibility built into this
design. Customizations are optional, and
not every item needs to have a topping
or a side. So even if a menu item has no
customizations, it can still be stored,
displayed, and priced properly. This
design supports both simple and complex
items equally. And this is just one of
the advanced database optimizations that
we teach over on jsmastery.com.

store. And all of this code is just
plain JavaScript. We are dealing with
adding and removing different items from
it and just checking whether it was
added before or not to make sure that we
have a robust cart system. Because
building a cart store requires a deep
understanding of how users interact with
e-commerce systems. The cart must be
able to handle a variety of different
actions like adding items, adjusting
quantities, or even including optional
customizations, all while maintaining
accurate totals. So, the goal is to
answer the core question, what defines a
unique cart item? And for a food
delivery app, it is not just a menu item
because a cheeseburger with extra cheese
or a cheeseburger with fries are two
different things because they have
different customizations.
So, we can't just track it by a menu ID.
Rather, we need a combination of a menu
ID plus customizations to identify an
item uniquely. So, at the center is an
items array right here that contains all
of our items. And here's a type of how
each one of these cart item types look
like. We have an ID, a name, a price,
image, URL, quantity, and then finally
an array of different cart
customizations, and they are optional
because not all items need them. There's
a plain Coke or margarita pizza that
doesn't have any add-ons. So, by making
it optional, the cart works for both
simple and complex options. And one of
the most important steps is to identify
unique items. And we do that using this
R customizations equal utility function.
As I said before, it basically combines
two different arrays, sorts them, and
then checks whether they're the same. So
for example, it ensures that burger,
fries, and coke is not treated the same
as burger, extra cheese, and fries, even
if both reference the same menu item.
Then of course we were on the add item
